二、编磬和编镈的现有音高如上表所示，并不足以揭示它们原有的音阶情况，其原因如下：

  1. 石磬一般情况下易于腐蚀、损毁; 编辑除个别略好的例子而外, 一般调音技术都极差。
  2. 这两套乐器又在最小的磬、最大的键上（这二者恰是判断音阶的关键部位）进行了修复而影响了对它原有音高的判断。
  3. 迄今为止, 编磬与编搏因上述两种带普遍性的原因, 只有个别例子略可判明音阶情况 (河南省博物馆藏陕县石磬和浙川编搏), 此外并无其他资料可供参考。
  4. 2040:3 石磬, 双孔, 可能为配套悬挂而调整位置者。此类并非原套的配件 (如箸篱钟的首钟), 其现有音高, 一般值得怀疑。2040:24 铜锛, 亦经修复, 其音高亦可能因附加金属而偏高。

三、这套编磬出于陕县后川，与河南省博物馆现存陕县编磬(9件编列)及晋西南侯马M-13编磬（7件）可能有相近的音阶形式：宫、角、微、羽、宫、商、角、微、羽、宫。

取墓2040出土编磬第一件、第五件为“宫”，求其平均音分值(465.89Hz。按440标准为 ${}^{\mathrm{b}}\mathbf{b}^{1} - 1,973.04\mathrm{Hz}$ ，则为 ${}^{\mathrm{b}}\mathbf{b}^{2} + 74$ )得 $+37$ 音分。取 $476.23\mathrm{Hz}$ 。（即440标准之 ${}^{\mathrm{b}}\mathbf{b}^{1}+$ 37）为该磬标准音 ${}^{\mathrm{b}}\mathbf{b}^{1}\pm 0$ ，可得其现有音高之音名、音分值如下：

馨序12345678910
2040:6:5:4:3:10:7:2:1:8:9
b b¹ - 38b d² - 24f² - 5g² - 17b b² + 37c³ + 42b d³ + 18f³ - 34g³ + 50(?!)

1）按墓2040出土编磬现有音高的相对关系，可以暂定其音阶序列的各个阶名（以十、一号数目、表明高于或低于此阶应有音高若干古代音差)如下：

磐序12345678910
羽-官-角变微羽+变官+宫角变微‡(羽?)

上述假定阶名之不合理有二：①迄今为止，未曾发现在编磬中出现使用二变的音阶。②即使该编磬是一种特殊的制度，也不应在五声不全的情况下出现二变。

2）可以估计，编磬原有的音高既有合理的高阶序列，又有一定相对准确高度。目前的音高不准，应该出于石灰质的腐蚀变薄或磬身损毁之故，据现有的测音数据，判断它原来的音阶关系，有下列两种可能：

第一种可能：以第四、七两磬腐蚀变薄固而音高变低为条件，它的原有音阶序列可能是：

12345678910
b1-38bd2-24f2-5ba2-117bd2+37bd2-58be3-182f3-34ba3-50
羽一宫一角微二羽+宫二商三角一微二(羽?)

第二种可能：以第二、七两磬腐蚀变薄因而音高变低为条件，它的原有音阶序列可能是：

12345678910
b1-38d2-124f2-5g2-17b2+37c3+42d3-82f3-34g3+50
官一j=—微羽宫+商+角一微一羽‡(宫?)

这两种可能，以第二种为接近正确。可以通过实物考察磐身变薄(“一”号增多者)磐身磨损变短(“+”号增多者)的情况来加验证。

当第二种可能属实时，可以得出这套编磬与河南省博物馆现藏陕县石磬宫音音高同为 $^{\text{b}}\mathsf{b}^{\text{I}}$ ，音阶序列基本相同的结论，亦可从而得出它们的制作年代亦基本相近的结论。

四、墓2040出土的编簿和大多数编簿一样，音高甚不准确(古人所谓“钟不和”)，不能像编磬一样来估计它们原有比较准确的音列。从可以考察编簿音阶的河南淅川下夺楚墓编簿(八件编列)对照研究时，略可判断这套编簿的最低音——一首钟，也可能是“微”音。其音高应在 $\mathbf{e}^{1}$ （修复后，由于增加了填料，音高提升为 $\mathbf{g}^{1}$ )。因此，宫音应在三、六、八各钟，音高应为A。

今舍去修复后的第三搏不计，取第六、八两搏为宫音，而计其平均音分值(892.8Hz。按440标准为 $a^2 + 25; 1805.3Hz$ 。则为 $a^3 + 44$ )得 $+34$ 音分。以 $448.73Hz$ 。（即440标准之 $a^1 + 34$ )为该编搏标准音 $a^1 \pm 0$ ，可估计编搏应有阶名、音名及其相对音分值如下：

键序123456789
2040:25:20:24:22:23:405:21:407:406
e1+259#f1+43a1+27#c2+12e2-44a2-9e3-156a3+10#f4-7
微羽+官+角微二官微三官羽

由于先秦钟属乐器之中, 镯顿晚出, 编辑制作更晚, 它又是“平于”的青铜乐器, 发音的振动状况与“非平于”的编钟并不完全相同; 因而在调音技术上的历史经验颇少, 致使调音不易准确。从这套编辑与编磬的关系看, 其原有设计意图, 锻磬也许同套; 但宫音音高统一按 $440\mathrm{Hz} = a^{1}$ 的标准来比较时, 一为 $a^{1} + 34$ , 一为 ${}^{\text{山}}\mathsf{b}^{\text{山}} + 37$ , 恰有半音之差, 如系相同年代制作, 则应为铸造及调音技术未能妥善所致。

附录三 陕县汉墓出土铁器的金相鉴定

中国社会科学院考古研究所实验室

陕县汉墓出土铁器经用金相方法检验了7件，其金相组织简述如下：

（1）铁釜(3101:10)、铁足铜鼎（3411:5）两者的金相组织均由渗碳体和莱氏体组成，含碳量在 $4.3\%$ 左右，是共晶白口铸件(图版一一一,3)。
(2) 铁钎(3003:109) 原样品已残断, 仅取其环首部分。金相组织为铁素体和珠光体, 组织不均匀, 含碳量在 $0.25 - 0.55\%$ , 夹杂物细少, 是由几块不同含碳量的炒钢为原料加热锻打而成(图版一一二, 4)。
（3）铁钎(3010:24）原样品已残断，取铁钎的中间部分。金相组织是铁素体和珠光体，从横截面观察，其一边含碳量 $0.2\%$ ，另一边含碳量 $0.5\%$ ，有细硅酸盐夹杂物，是由两块炒钢原料锻打而成(图版一一一，4)。
（4）铁剑(3007:12）通长111.8厘米。从剑端取样，样品锈蚀严重，经磨样后仅在横截面残存一小块金属，其余均腐蚀。金相组织是马氏体基体，局部有上贝氏体(羽毛状)，含碳量 $0.4 - 0.5\%$ ，组织均匀，有细小点状氧化物夹杂，数量较少，铁剑成形后经过淬火处理(图版一一二,1)。
（5）铁剑(3010:44)通长117.5厘米。从剑端残部截取样品，金相组织是铁素体和珠光体，中心部分高低碳层相间为六层，含碳量分别为 $0.25\%$ 和 $0.5\%$ ，每层成分均匀。剑的表面含碳量高，约 $0.7\%$ ，有细碎硅酸盐夹杂物较多，变形量较大，这些夹杂物沿加工方向排列成行。铁剑是以不同含碳量的炒钢为原料反复加热锻打而成(图版一一二,2)。
（6）铁剑(3027:27）原样品已残断，在剑端部截取试样。金相组织因含碳量不同分为十五层，由高碳层和中碳层相间组成，最低含碳量约 $0.35\%$ ，最高含碳量约 $0.65\%$ 。高碳层为板条状，金相组织是马氏体和屈氏体，有少量上贝菌体(图版一一二,3)。中碳部分为铁素体及细珠光体组织，边部含碳更低。夹杂物系硅酸盐为主，铁的氧化物数量较少。是由几块不同含碳量的炒钢为原料经过加热锻打而成。

以上金相鉴定表明, 除铁釜和铜鼎铁足是共晶白口铁铸件外, 其余 5 件都是钢制品。两件铁剑(3007:12、3027:27)是经过淬火处理的。这些钢制品是以含碳量不同的炒钢作为原料经过反复多次锻打而成的。铁剑(3007:12)出土于西汉初期墓中, 这说明我国在西汉初期已出现了炒钢技术。该剑是迄今发现的我国最早炒钢制品, 在冶金史研究上具有重要意义。

执笔者 杜弗运

本报告系由叶小燕和支沅洪整理编写，并由叶小燕通阅定稿。绘图由曹继秀、张心石、冯振慧、刘莉、陈江浩担任。器物照相由赵钰、姜言忠负责，刘国强也参加了部分器物的拍摄。器物的修整工作有刘增堃、丁六龙等同志参加。全报告经苏秉琦先生审阅，黄展岳、孟凡人同志也提出过有益的意见。

1987年5月

TOMBS OF THE EASTERN ZHOU, QIN AND HAN DYNASTIES IN SHAANXIAN COUNTY

Tombs of the Eastern Zhou, Qin and Han Dynasties at Houchuan Village in Shaanxian County of western Henan Province were excavated from 1956 to 1958; altogether 234 graves were exposed, of which 107 are dated to the Eastern Zhou (including 2 horse-and-chariot burials), 92 to the Qin and the early Han, and 35 to the Han Dynasty.

All the tombs of the Eastern Zhou Dynasty are rectangular shaft pits, most facing north. They have a single or double wooden coffin, sometimes encased in a wooden chamber, the space between the walls of the chamber and those of the pit, in some large graves, being filled up with pebbles, with charcoal added occasionally. Most of the tombs are found to have extended supine burials, and flexed supine ones also occur in some cases. There are two tombs either with a human victim. Funeral objects, apart from ornaments and part of weapons, are mostly put in chambers.

These tombs are divided into two types: tombs mainly with pottery and tombs mainly with bronzes. Two of the former yielded guan vases, dou stemmed vessels, pen basins and li tripods (Fig. 16). In cultural characteristic and date, they are identical with the Guo State cemetery at Shangcunling. The rest of this sort of tombs are subdivided into three groups (for their pottery, see Plates XI; XII—XIV; XVI—XXV) dated respectively to the middle and late Spring and Autumn Period, the early Warring States period, and the times covering the middle Warring States period and the time prior to the Qin State's taking Shaan in 325 BC. The tombs of the latter type are subdivided into two groups (for their bronzes, see Plates XXVI, XXVII; XXVIII—XXXI). They can be dated respectively to the middle and late Spring and Autumn Period and the early Warring States period plus a slightly later time.

Among these graves the largest one is Tomb 2040. It contains a double wooden coffin inside the chamber, outside which the space being filled up with pebbles and charcoal. The funeral objects number nearly 2000, including a bronze dagger-axe inlaid with the inscription 'Zi Kong 子孔' in gold. The tomb-owner must be a member of the high-ranking aristocracy of that time. Typologically, the grave goods are similar to those of the second group of tombs with bronzes (Plates XXXIII---XLV). The date of the tomb is inferred to be roughly in the early Warring States period.

The group of Qin and early Han Dynasties tombs includes burials dated from the late Warring States period after the Qin State's taking Shaan to the early years of the Western Han Dynasty. They are either rectangular pits or earthen caves with shaft entrances, most facing west. The dead were generally buried in

wooden coffins, mostly in a flexed position and occasionally in an extended supine position. There is an example of the couple's joint burial. Most of the funeral objects are put outside the coffin, and ornaments, knives and swords are seen on skeletons.

According to the difference in the combination of funeral objects (see Plates LXVII, LXVIII; LXIX—LXXXI), these tombs are divided into two groups. In the first group, the grave goods are comparatively small in number and similar in form and variety to those from the Qin tombs of the late Warring States period at Banpo near Xi'an, and the tombs themselves are also identical with the latter burials in the shape of the pits and the flexed position of the dead, but different from those of the same period at Luoyang. These give reasons to infer that they belonged to Qin people who moved there after the Qin State taking Shaan and that they can be dated back to the late Warring States period. The second group of tombs contain funeral objects similar in form and variety to those from the tombs of the Qin and early Han Dynasties in Yaoxian County, Shaanxi; Mixian County, Henan; and at Shuihudi of Yunmeng, Hubei. They exhibit distinct features of the Qin culture and should be considered a continuation of the first group of tombs. Ban-liang coins of the reign of Han Emperor Wendi were found in some cases. Thus the tombs can be dated back to the reigns of early Han Emperors Wendi and Jingdi.

The tombs of the Han Dynasty include catacombs with a shaft entrance or a sloping passage. The dead were buried in wooden coffins, in an extended supine position. As the joint burial was quite prevalent at that time, even as many as five skeletons are found in the same tomb, though single burials are also seen. Grave goods are mostly put in the front of the chamber, on a side of the coffin and in the side cabin, but small objects are seen on skeletons.

According to the difference in tomb form and funeral objects (see Plates LXXXIX-XCI, CI-CVII; XCII-XCIV, CVIII; XCV; XCV-C, CVIII), these tombs may be divided into four groups, which are dated respectively to the reign of Western Han Emperor Wudi and a little later, the later Western Han Dynasty and the period of the New Government of Wang Mang, the earlier period of the Eastern Han, and the later period of this dynasty.

Included in the present report are 1404 pottery, 1579 bronze, 118 iron, 12 lead, 35 broken lequered (only their metal ornaments being left), 4048 jade and stone and 2863 bone, ivory and shell objects as well as 2545 coins of the Eastern Zhou and Qin-Han periods.

Shaanxian lies between Xi'an and Luoyang. During the early and middle Warring States period, it was part of the territory of Wei State, and the Warring States period cemetery here must have belong to Wei people. The tomb form and the funeral objects exhibit the cultural style of the Central Plains. The Qin tombs of the late Warring States period were built roughly after Qin King Heiwenwang taking Shaan in 325 BC. Their burial rites and cultural traits are basically the same as those of the tombs in the Qin State, but certain features show that the Qin culture began to merge with that of the Central Plains. Therefore, the Qin and early Han tombs in Shaanxian constitute a batch of rather complete and typical data for researching into this process, and have important academic value in the study

of the Qin culture. The Han tombs here are similar to those at Liujiau (see the Yellow River Reservoirs Archaeological Team, 'Excavations of the Han Tombs at Liu Chia Ch'u in Shanhsien, Honan Province', Kaogu Xuebao, No. 1, 1965). Their cultural style tends to that of the Xi'an area rather than that of the Luoyang region. However, in the later Eastern Han Dynasty, the difference between all of them was gradually disappearing and finally replaced by uniformity.
